Presentation and Consideration of the FY 2022-2023 Recommended Budget

Ladies and Gentlemen:
In accordance with the Henderson City Charter and the Statutes of the State of North Carolina, the FY 202223 Recommended Budget is herewith submitted to the City Council for its review, deliberation, amendments
and adjustments as deemed appropriate, and adoption prior to July 1st. The City Administration looks
forward to working with the Mayor and members of City Council on the budget during the next several
weeks, and helping it achieve a spending plan which addresses the needs and aspirations of both the
community and municipal organization.
OVERVIEW
State law requires localities adopt and operate within a balanced budget at all times. To that end, revenues
have shown some growth, but continue to be projected in a conservative manner and expenditures have been
limited to capital needs. Revenues in the Enterprise Funds continue to remain stable and there have been
increases in real and personal property values. FY 21 collections trended up and FY 22 is pacing ahead of
last year which positively affects the General Fund projected tax revenue.
Many projects have reached completion and other initiatives such as the outdoor pavilion are in the
construction phase which will further our goals for downtown development.
With the COVID-19 virus largely behind us, the City can continue to seek every opportunity to strengthen
our economic base.

BM: Page 1 of 6

GENERAL FUND
The General Fund recommended budget for FY 2022-23 is $22,688,450.
Revenues
•

Personal property and real property tax is gaining value, and signs of new growth are taking
shape. The tax rate is recommended to remain at .712 per $100 of property value.
 2021 City Value (property, personal, utilities, and gap) -$827,167,890
 2022 City Value (property, personal, utilities, and gap) -839,213,376

•

The tax collection rate reported in our FY 21 increased to 97.59% from the previous year of
95.78% and collections for FY 22 are tracking ahead of last year.

•

Revenues from sales tax and other hold harmless taxes have continued to surpass previous
year’s marks despite the period of time attributable to the COVID virus.

•

Appropriation of $1,675,000 of Fund Balance. This is a considerable reduction from what
was originally recommended last fiscal year due to the availability of ARPA funds used to
balance the budget. Per the City's 2021 audit, the fund balance remained sound and well
above the minimum requirements as set by the Local Government Commission (LGC).

•

As part of the budget process, no changes in the schedule of fees and revenues are
expected for FY 23.

•

Provided that the City will eventually establish a storm-water utility fee, this will help fund
much needed stormwater infrastructure projects for future budgets.

Highlights on the expenditure side of the General Fund are as follows:
•

With the appropriation of ARPA funds, CIP/Redevelopment initiatives can receive a priority
focus for the City where $2,476,000 has been earmarked for a variety of paving, building
renovations, and redevelopment pursuits.

BM: Page 2 of 6

•

The Police Department law enforcement officers currently receive a 5.0% 401-K match
as mandated by state law. Salary enhancements have been put into place to improve
retention and longevity.

•

Downtown Development Commission (DDC) - $25,000 is proposed to pay for continued
funding of downtown development incentives and grants.

•

Capital outlay of equipment in the Police Department and Fire Department is a combination
of loan purchase and outright purchase:

•

Police Department — replacement vehicles — to be loan purchased over a five- year
period with the first payment due in FY 24. Radios, computers, and vehicle upfit items
are budgeted for equipment.

•

Information Technology — Cyber security/data storage.

•

Fire Department – New fire engine.

•

Development Services - UDO

•

Public Works – HVAC replacement and ice machine (shared).

•

Recreation – HVAC replacement.

•

Water Reclamation – Pump/SCADA IT

•

Sewer Collection – HVAC replacement and ice machine (shared).

•

Includes funding of $100,000 for Economic Development Incentive grants due to
existing agreements with M R Williams, North Central Medical Transport, and MAKO
Laboratory.

•

Funding for outside local agencies such as Crime Stoppers, Boys and Girls Club and Arts
Council matches FY 21 levels.

BM: Page 3 of 6

REGIONAL WATER
The Regional Water Fund Budget is proposed at $5,312,490; a slight increase from the current year budget.
Regional Water production has remained steady throughout the past fiscal year.
Expense highlights are as follows:
•

No rate increase is proposed.

• Funds in the amount of $1,850,000 will be placed in Regional Water Reserve.
• Capital Outlay in the total amount of $107,000 for lab work and pumps.
WATER FUND
The Water Fund budget is proposed at $7,530,571 which is a slight decrease from the past year. Highlights
of the Water Fund are as follows:
• No rate increase is proposed.
• Capital Outlay — (Water) HVAC replacement and ice machine shared with Sewer and
Public Works.

SEWER FUND
The Sewer Fund budget is proposed at $5,567,907 which is a slight increase from the past year. No use of
Rate Stabilization funds is proposed to be used this year.
Some highlights of the expense side include funding for the following:
•

No rate increase is proposed.

•

Capital outlay for replacement pumps and SCADA IT software, HVAC replacement
and ice machine (shared).

BM: Page 4 of 6

EMPLOYEE PAY AND OTHER BENEFITS CONSIDERED IN THE FY 22 BUDGET:
Personnel - Salaries and wages continue to be a major concern for the City in many departments as we must
find ways to increase our competitiveness to hire and retain the best staff.
Health insurance — The City maintains its association with insurance broker, Mark III, as negotiations
continue to establish the FY 23 benefits.
Proposed Monthly Premium of $632.87 per Employee with $5,000 deductible – With an increase in
our HRA offering from up to $1,000 (after $2,000 met) to up to $1,500 (after $3,500 met) due to an increase
in deductible.
Worker's Compensation - The City has received an estimated premium for Worker's Compensation
insurance rates from the League of Municipalities which represents a decrease from last year and lower
than what has been initially budgeted.
Debt — Other than the KLRW plant upgrade, which will increase the debt service in the future, and current
long-term projects already forecasted for years to come, no other major projects have been added to our debt
facility. Capital outlay loans will reduce this year with another significant retirement of debt in FY 23.
Combining all funds, this represents a combined budget of $43,747,353. Whereas not all needs are met, as
in most budgets, I believe this budget represents further positive steps in areas not funded previously which
will help the City continue to grow and reach its full potential. We must continue to work towards innovative
ways to promote and energize redevelopment initiatives now to position the City to be ready for near-future
business prospects and citizenship.
Again, I appreciate the opportunity to work for the City of Henderson in providing a high level of service that our
citizens deserve and look forward to working with the staff in carrying out the necessary work to ensure that our
goals are realized as outlined in the Strategic Plan.

I want to take this opportunity to especially thank our Finance department staff. I am extremely grateful in the
team's efforts in putting this budget together as well as the cooperation of the Department Directors.
I welcome your suggestions and comments as we prepare for our Budget Hearing as well as upcoming meetings,
as needed, to discuss this proposed budget.
Sincerely,

Terrell Blackmon, City Manager
City of Henderson

BUDGET GUIDE

The FY 2022-23 Budget has been prepared in a reader friendly, program-oriented budget format. The
following summary provides information on how the reader might best understand the budget by first
explaining the format of the budget.
The budget describes recommended City services and revenue sources proposed for the fiscal year
beginning 1 July 2022 and ending 30 June 2023. Adopting an annual budget is one of the most important
tasks the Mayor and City Council undertake each year. Indeed, it is the single most important policy
document that the Mayor and Council will approve. It is through the adoption and implementation of the
budget that the interests and values of our community are translated into plans for and the service delivery
of programs, projects, services, and resources intended to benefit the citizens of Henderson.
1. This book is divided into sections.
2. Read the Budget Calendar found on page BG-8. All budget work sessions are open to the public
and the public is cordially invited to attend.
3. Read the Budget Process and Accounting Policies beginning on page BG-2.
4. This document provides information on the overall budget and identifies major policy
recommendations and shifts in policy direction found within the budget document.
5. Read the Program Narratives for each budget division. These narratives will provide the reader
with information about the division’s purpose and budget highlights.
6. Read the Glossary found in Appendix A. The Glossary provides definitions for various words and
phrases used within the budget that may not be generally understood by the lay reader.
7. If you have any questions about the budget, please feel free to contact the City Manager at (252)
430-5701.
BUDGET ADOPTION
The City of Henderson’s annual operating budget is adopted by ordinance in accordance with the North
Carolina Local Government Budget and Fiscal Control Act (LGBFCA) which states that estimated revenues
and appropriated fund balances equal appropriations. The budget is developed on a cash basis, including
only expenditures and revenues expected to be realized during the fiscal year. State statute also sets the
fiscal year as beginning July 1 and ending June 30: therefore, the City Council must adopt a budget prior
to July 1st of each year.
ACCOUNTING POLICIES
Summary
The accounting policies of the City of Henderson conform to generally accepted accounting principles
(GAAP) as applicable to government units. The Governmental Accounting Standards Board (GASB) is
the accepted standard-setting body for establishing governmental accounting and financial reporting
principles. The following is a summary of the more significant accounting policies:
Reporting Entity
The City of Henderson is a Municipal Corporation governed by an elected mayor and an eight-member
council.

BG: Page 2

Fund Accounting
The accounts of the City are organized and operate on the basis of funds and account groups. A fund is an
independent fiscal and accounting entity with a self-balancing set of accounts comprised of assets,
liabilities, fund equity, revenues, and expenditures or expenses, as appropriate. Fund accounting segregates
funds according to their intended purpose and is used to aid management in demonstrating compliance with
finance-related legal and contractual provisions. The minimum number of funds is maintained consistent
with legal and managerial requirements. The account groups are not funds but are a reporting device used
to account for certain assets and liabilities of the governmental funds that are not recorded directly in those
funds. The City uses the following fund categories:
Governmental Funds
Governmental Funds are used to account for the City's governmental functions. Governmental funds
include the following fund types:
General Fund - The General Fund is the general operating fund of the City. It is used to account for all
financial resources except those required to be accounted for in another fund. The primary revenue sources
are ad valorem taxes and State-shared revenues. The primary expenditures are for public safety, streets,
sanitation, parks and recreation, and general government services.
Powell Bill Fund - The Powell Bill Fund serves as a transfer account for expenditures incurred with Powell
Bill Street allocations for accounting purposes.
Grant Revenue Funds – The Grant Revenue Funds are used to account for the proceeds of specific revenue
sources (other than special assessments, expendable trusts, or major capital projects) that are legally
restricted to expenditures for specified purposes.
Capital Projects Funds - Capital Projects Funds account for financial resources to be used for the acquisition
and construction of major capital facilities (other than those financed by proprietary funds and trust funds).
The City has two Capital Projects funds within its governmental fund types, the General Capital Projects
Fund, and the Powell Bill Capital Projects Fund.
Proprietary Funds
Enterprise Fund - The Enterprise Fund is used to account for operations (a) that are financed and operated
in a manner similar to private business enterprises - where the intent of the governing body is that the costs
(expenses, including depreciation) of providing goods and services to the general public on a continuing
basis be financed or recovered primarily through user charges; or (b) where the governing body has decided
that periodic determination of revenues earned, expenses incurred, and/or net income is appropriate for
capital maintenance, public policy, management control, accountability, or other purposes. The Enterprise
Fund includes the Water Fund, Regional Water System Fund, Sewer Fund, Capital Reserve Utilities Fund,
Capital Reserve Regional Fund, and Water, Sewer, and Regional Water Capital Project Funds.
Fiduciary Fund
Pension Trust Fund - The Pension Trust Fund accounts for the Law Enforcement Officers' Special
Separation Allowance enacted by the State Legislature and effective January 1, 1987.

BG: Page 3

BASIS OF BUDGETING
In accordance with the North Carolina General Statutes, the City is required to budget and account for its
funds on the modified accrual basis of accounting. Under this method, revenues are recognized in the
accounting period in which they become available. Expenditures are recognized in the accounting period
in which the goods and services are received (except for unmatured interest on general long-term debt,
which is recognized when due). The City’s accounting records for general governmental operations are
reported on the modified accrual basis. Enterprise and pension trust operations are reported on the accrual
basis.
The General Statutes also provide for balanced project ordinances for the life of the projects, including both
capital and grant activities, which are expected to extend beyond the end of the fiscal year. The budgeted
appropriations for capital and grant projects do not lapse until the completion of the project, while
appropriations for funds that adopt annual budgets lapse at the end of the fiscal year.
In keeping with state law, the budget is prepared and accounted for on a modified accrual basis for the
general fund, enterprise funds, and the Powell Bill fund. All other funds, including capital project and grant
funds adopt multi-year budgets.
In keeping with Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP), financial statements for proprietary
funds are prepared on a full accrual basis. A reconciliation of the full accrual basis to modified accrual
basis (budgetary basis) is included in the annual financial statements for enterprise funds.
Budget Structure
Fund Accounting, required for governmental units, is the style of accounting used to segregate and account
for restricted resources. The City of Henderson develops its source and use of funds estimates contained
in the annual budget in a manner that follows general accepted accounting principles (GAAP). The budget
is organized on the basis of funds, each of which is considered a separate budgetary and accounting entity.
Governmental resources are accounted for in individual funds based upon the purposes for which they are
to be spent.
The primary focuses of most budget documents are the revenue and expenditure appropriations. The City's
operating expenditures are organized into the following hierarchy: Funds, Departments, Categories and
Line Items. To better understand the budget documents, the terms in the City's financial structure should
be understood.
FUNDS
The budget is organized on the basis of funds, each of which is considered a separate budgetary and
accounting entity. Government resources are allocated to, and accounted for, in individual funds based
upon the purposes for which they are to be spent.
A.

OPERATING BUDGET - The City of Henderson's annual budget is adopted as required by the
North Carolina General Statutes. The annual budget is divided into twelve separate funds as
indicated below:

BG: Page 4

Governmental Funds
10: General Fund - handles the general operations of the City such as administration, police
protection, fire services, planning/zoning, recreational programs, street maintenance, sanitation
services and Elmwood Cemetery operations. Resources are provided primarily through ad valorem
taxes and intergovernmental revenues and are expended for services which are not compatible with
a user fee financing method.
11: Powell Bill Fund - serves as a transfer account to properly account for expenditures incurred
with Powell Bill Street Allocations. This annual State allocation is restricted to street, sidewalk
and right-of-way maintenance, resurfacing and the purchase of equipment directly related to streets
and right-of-ways. Transfers are made monthly to the General Fund or Capital Improvement
Project Fund for the expenses incurred for these specific uses.
Enterprise Funds
30: Water Enterprise Fund - accounts for the provision of water services to utility customers and
the revenue associated with the distribution of this water service. This enterprise fund is operated
in a manner similar to private business enterprise. This fund is fully self-supporting by user charges
for its service.
31: Sewer Enterprise Fund - provides for the activities necessary to furnish sewer treatment,
sanitary sewer collection and inflow and infiltration of sewer. This enterprise fund is operated in a
manner similar to private business enterprise. This fund is fully self-supporting by user charges
for its service.
64: Regional Water System Fund - accounts for all revenue and expenditure sources to the City's
Kerr Lake Regional Water System which is under the control of Henderson (60%), Oxford (20%)
and Warren County (20%). The City of Henderson as the majority and controlling partner operates
the system which serves over 40,000 customers for the joint use and benefit of all three entities and
their respective service areas.
Capital Reserve Funds
70: Capital Reserve Utilities Fund - provides for the accumulation of revenues for future expansion
and/or construction of water and sewer system and related improvements.
72: Capital Reserve General Fund - provides for the accumulation of revenues for future large
capital equipment purchases and/or construction of facilities normally funded through General
Fund resources including infrastructure components such as streets, sidewalks, storm drainage
systems, public buildings, etc.
73: Capital Reserve Economic Development Fund - provides for a small reserve for economic
development grant matches and incentives. It has no fund balance since the fund is just now being
established.
78: Capital Reserve Regional Water Fund - consolidates three Regional Capital Reserve Funds
into one. 75: Capital Reserve Regional Water COE, 76: Capital Reserve Regional Water 20 MGD
and 77: Capital Reserve Regional General.

BG: Page 5

79: Capital Reserve Rate Stabilization Fund - was established in March 2012 for the purpose of
reserving money for rate stability while performing potential upgrades in the water and sewer
systems.
B.

CAPITAL IMPROVEMENT PROJECTS FUND BUDGETS - The Capital Improvement Projects
fund budgets account for financial resources to be used for the acquisition and construction of major
capital facilities (other than those financed by proprietary funds and trust funds). The City has two
Capital Projects Funds within its governmental fund type (General and Powell Bill), and three
Capital Projects Funds within its proprietary fund type (Water, Sewer & Regional Water). Capital
Improvement Projects Fund projects are not considered to be part of the annual budget process
since these projects remain “open” for the life of the project.
Governmental Funds
41: General Capital Improvement Projects Fund - accounts for the costs of general projects
currently in the planning and/or construction stage.
42: Powell Bill Capital Improvement Projects Fund - accounts for the costs of Powell Bill projects
currently in the planning and/or constructions stage.
Proprietary Funds
43: Water Capital Improvements Projects Fund - accounts for the costs of water projects currently
in the planning and/or construction stage.
44: Sewer Capital Improvements Projects Fund - accounts for the costs of sewer projects currently
in the planning and/or construction stage.
46: Regional Water Improvements Projects Fund - accounts for the costs of Regional Water
projects currently in the planning and/or construction stage.

C.

GRANT PROJECT FUNDS - Grant Project Funds are projects that are set up by project ordinances
that are funded in full or in part by Federal or State funds and have more than one year duration.

EXPENDITURE CLASSIFICATION
Departments are divisions of a fund that are responsible for performing specific government functions
within their area of accountability. The departments represent the highest level of summarization used in
the City's budget structure. Each department is divided by department budget as noted below:
Fund Department
A.

B.

General Fund: Governing Body, City Attorney, Administration, Human Resources, Finance,
Development Services, Henderson-Vance Downtown Development, Public Buildings, Bennett
Perry House, IT Services, Police, Fire, Admin/Public Works, Garage, Cemetery, Street, Sanitation,
Recreation, Recreation/Youth Services, Aycock Aquatics Center, Non-Departmental, Vance
County Shared Programs, Contribution to Local Agencies and Debt Service.
Water Fund: Engineering, Water Distribution, Administrative, Customer Service, NonDepartmental and Debt Service.

Regional Water Fund: Water Treatment, Non-Departmental and Debt Service.
Capital Reserve: Utilities, General Fund, Economic Development, Regional Water and Rate
Stabilization

DEPARTMENTAL EXPENDITURES CATEGORIES
Each department is broken down into four categories:
A.
B.
C.
D.

Personnel Services includes salaries and wages (full-time and part-time), pensions, health
insurance, merit and other fringe benefits.
Operating pertains to the daily operations that provide basic governmental services such as
supplies, utilities, materials and travel.
Debt Service includes appropriated principal and interest payments for any outstanding debt within
each funds department.
Capital Outlay includes funds for the purchase of equipment, land or other fixed assets valued at
more than $1,000 with a lifespan of at least 5 years.

LINE ITEMS
Each budget consists of individual line items which identify specific expenditures and revenues. Line items
are the most detailed way to list budget information.
BUDGET AMENDMENTS
Throughout the fiscal year, adjustments to the original budget ordinance may become necessary to meet
changing circumstances, better carry out planned programs and provide for new Council initiatives. Two
types of changes can occur – budget amendments and budget transfers. Such changes are required at the
line-item level of the budget underlying the budget ordinance.
A budget amendment increases or decreases the revenue and expenditures of an appropriated fund (this
may involve decreases or increases in revenue and expenditures or shifting of monies between funds).
Budget amendments, in accordance with North Carolina Statute 159-15, require the approval of City
Council. Amendment requests are submitted to the Finance Department and approved by the City Manager
prior to submittal to City Council.
BUDGET TRANSFERS
A budget transfer changes line-item appropriations within a particular fund without changing the fund total.
Interdepartmental budget transfers are prepared by the Department Directors. All transfer actions are
reviewed and approved by both the Finance Director and the City Manager.
BUDGET CALENDAR
The City of Henderson begins its annual budget process early in the fiscal year, with the distribution of the
Capital Improvements Program (a ten year planning document), and the annual operating budget
instructions and forms to Department Directors and Local Agencies.

BG: Page 7

The Department Directors have approximately three (3) months to concentrate on developing goals and
objectives, requesting expenses and justifying any capital outlay for their department for the upcoming
fiscal year.
After reviewing initial development requests, the City Manager and select staff meet with Department
Directors. These informal meetings allow Department Heads to fully explain their requests and respond to
questions from the City Manager. After these review meetings, expenditure and revenue decisions are
made and line item recommendations are put into draft form, and presented to the Mayor, City Council and
Department Directors.
Several Council/Staff work sessions are held that focus on the Operating and CIP Budgets for the upcoming
year. Council directives are stated during these meetings and then incorporated into each budget document.
A public hearing on the proposed budget is held in June prior to budget adoption. Citizen concerns are
voiced to the Mayor and Council at this public hearing. The budget is adopted at the City Council’s last
regular meeting in June or at a special called meeting set for the purpose of adopting the budget subsequent
to the Budget hearing.

Executive Summary:
Consensus Achieved
The FY 22-23 Recommended Budget was presented to Council on May 9, 2022 and
discussed further during several work sessions along with the Public Hearing held on
May 23, 2022. A summary of the comments and discussion from the Council may be
found in the Council Minutes. There were no comments received during the Public
Hearing, so Council continued to deliberate on the budget.
After review of an assortment of budget items and overall recap of the total budget, there
was a consensus of the Council to recommend approval of the FY 23 budget during the
Regularly Scheduled meeting on Monday, June 13, 2022.

ORDINANCE 22-10
APPROVAL OF
THE FY 22-23 ANNUAL OPERATING BUDGET,
ESTABLISHING THE 2022 AD VALOREM PROPERTY TAX RATE
AND
APPROVING THE ANNUAL FEE SCHEDULE
WHEREAS, pursuant to North Carolina General Statutes 159-13, the State of North
Carolina requires its local governments to operate on a 1 July – 30 June fiscal
year basis; and
WHEREAS, said State law further requires local governments adopt a balanced budget
not later than 1 July; and
WHEREAS, the City Manager presented the FY 22-23 Recommended Budget to the City
Council on Monday, 9 May 2022 and subsequent to the Budget Presentation, City
Council met for one budget work session to discuss the budget and to make
changes as it deemed appropriate and necessary for the public health, safety and
welfare; and
WHEREAS, the City Council asked for comments from the public during its duly
advertised Budget Hearing on Monday, 23 May 2022; however, no one spoke for
or against the proposed budget; and
WHEREAS, the City Council, after no public comments were offered throughout the
budget review process and Budget Hearing, and deliberating the various
budgetary issues, achieved consensus on the FY 22-23 Budget.
NOW, THEREFORE BE IT ORDAINED by the Henderson City Council that it does
hereby approve:
•

The FY 22-23 Budget; and

•

Establishes the 2022 Ad Valorem property tax rate; and

•

No Increases to the Water, Sewer, Regional Water, or Sanitation rates to
its regular and governmental customers; and

•

Approves the Annual Fee Schedule;

as set forth in the following sections:

PART 2: ESTABLISHING THE 2022 PROPERTY TAX RATE
Section 4: Ad Valorem Property Tax Rate Established
There is hereby levied an Ad Valorem property tax rate of $0.712 on each one hundred
dollars ($100) valuation of taxable property, both real and personal, as listed for taxes as
of 1 January 2022 for the purpose of raising the revenue set forth in the 10: General Fund
budget estimates in order to finance its authorized appropriations necessary for the proper
running of the government and delivery of its services to citizens and customers. The
2022 Ad Valorem property tax revenue is based on an estimated overall valuation of
$906,576,121 at a collection rate of 97.59%.

PART 4: SPECIAL AUTHORIZATIONS
Section 9: Special Authorizations
The City Manager shall be authorized to re-allocate departmental appropriations among
the various objects of expenditures within any budget department as he deems
appropriate and necessary. Additionally, he shall be authorized to re-allocate
departmental appropriations among other departments within the same fund via an intrafund transfer as he deems appropriate and necessary. Notation of such appropriations
shall be made to the City Council on the next financial report. All intra-fund transfers
must also be approved in advance by the Finance Director.
Section 10: Restrictions
•

The inter-fund transfer of funds shall be made only with the prior consent of City
Council via a formal Budget Ordinance Amendment.

•

Contingency funds shall only be appropriated with the prior consent of City
Council via a formal Budget Ordinance Amendment.

•

Funds may only be appropriated from the Capital Reserve funds 70: Utilities
Capital Reserve Fund; 72: General Capital Reserve Fund; 73: Economic
Development Capital Reserve Fund; 78: Regional Water Capital Reserve Fund;
and 79: Rate Stabilization Capital Reserve Fund; with the prior consent of the
City Council via a formal Budget Ordinance Amendment.

Section 11: Capital Project and Grant Project Funds
•

The City Manager may recommend to the City Council the establishment of any
new capital project and or grant project or recommend amendments to existing
capital project and/or grant project via a formal Budget Ordinance Amendment.

•

Capital projects and grant projects are considered to be open for the life of the
project, or grant, and shall be reported to City Council in the same manner in
which regular financial reports are provided.

•

Once a capital/grant project is authorized by City Council, the City Manager may
treat said project as though it were any other operating fund provided if such
actions are consistent with project/grant restrictions and guidelines.

Section 12: Utilization of Budget and Budget Ordinance
This Ordinance, 22-23, adopts the FY22-23 Budget and said Budget shall be the basis of
the financial plan for the City of Henderson for the period beginning 1 July 2022 and
ending 30 June 2023. The City Manager shall administer guidance and direction to the
Department Directors and/or other duly authorized staff to implement their appropriate
CAF 22-55: 13 June 2022 Council Meeting
Page 15 of 34

portion of the Budget. Copies of the Budget Ordinance and its appropriate detail shall be
provided to all Department Directors. The Finance Director shall establish records
consistent with the Budget and its authorizing Ordinance and in compliance with the
North Carolina General Statutes and rules and regulations as may exist or be promulgated
in the future by the North Carolina Local Government Commission.
PART 5: FEE SCHEDULE AFFIRMED, AMENDED AND ADOPTED
Section 13: Fee Schedule Affirmed, Amended and Adopted
The Annual Fee Schedule incorporates the rates and fees associated with the various
charges for services and penalties for violations of City Codes. The Annual Fee Schedule
is more fully articulated in Attachment A to this Ordinance and is hereby reaffirmed and
adopted as part of this Ordinance.
PART 6: APPROVAL OF CAPITAL IMPROVEMENTS PLANS
Section 14: Utilities Multi-Year Capital Improvements Plan
The Utilities Multi-Year Capital Improvements Plan (CIP) is included for the period 2018
– 2028. This multi-year capital improvements plan is for the Regional Water, Water and
Sewer systems and said CIP is on permanent file in the Office of the City Clerk and is
incorporated herein as part of this Ordinance by reference.
Section 15: General Fund and Powell Bill Multi-Year Capital Improvements Plan
The CIP for the above-mentioned funds are included, but is modified with new projects
and adjustments to the timelines as necessary.
PART 7: ADOPTION OF BUDGET AND RELATED AUTHORIZING
INSTRUMENTS
Section 16: Effective Dates
All sections of this Ordinance shall become effective on 1 July 2022.

Chapter 23 (Weeded Lot)
Sec. 23-8. - Costs of abatement; when due; lien.
The cost incurred by the city in removing or otherwise remedying a public nuisance shall
be charged to the owner(s) of the lot or parcel of land. The costs incurred by the city shall
be as set out below and are designed to include an administrative cost reflecting the
typical time spent on processing these complaints through various stages in the
proceedings, and an implementation cost reflecting the time spent in clearing the lot or
otherwise removing the nuisance; a reimbursement for costs of notice and publication,
and an allocation for delinquent accounts under this chapter.
These costs are set out by the stage of the proceeding which is reached or the service is
rendered and are as follows. The costs under each of the categories, to wit, administrative
costs, implementation costs, etc., are to be added together.

R-Tier 1 Deposit is the deposit required by any new residential customer establishing an
account and not able to provide a satisfactory letter of good payment history from his/her most
recent water/sewer utility provider. Additionally, any existing residential customer as of the
effective date of this Ordinance (1 April 2013) whose service is subject to disconnection
because of non-payment will be required to increase his/her security deposit to the R-Tier 1
Deposit rate in order to re-establish service.
(The Social Security number is collected from any person who may become a debtor for purposes of Setoff Debt Collection, G.S §
105A-3(c). This information may be used for collection procedures. The disclosure of the social security number is not
mandatory; however, if a Social Security Card is not presented, the customer may be required to pay an additional deposit fee as
stated in the Schedule of fees.)

R-Tier 2 Deposit is the deposit required of any existing residential customer whose service has
been subject to disconnection for non-payment twice within a rolling 18-month period. Such
customers will have to increase their security deposit on-file to 2.5 times their average monthly
bill in order to have service restored. Additionally, if any person seeking to establish service
with the City is found to have an arrears balance from prior service with the City, he/she shall
be required to establish a security deposit equal to 2.5 times an average residential monthly bill,
even if he/she can provide a letter of good payment history from his/her most recent
water/sewer utility provider.

$1,675,000 of Fund Balance has been appropriated to balance this year’s budget to help cover
staff increases, insurance increases and other operational expenses, however projected savings
and revenue results typically negate the use of fund balance.
$1,341,100 has been budgeted for loan purchase of capital outlay to include a new Fire engine
and Police vehicles.
$1,325,100 has been budgeted for County shared programs to cover the City’s portion of
Elections, Tax Office, 911, H. Leslie Perry Library, and NCDMV.
$35,050 has been budgeted for Local Agencies such as Vance/Granville Airport, Corbitt Assn.,
Crime Stoppers, Boys & Girls Club, and the Vance Arts Council.
$25,000 has been budgeted for the contributions to the Downtown Development Commission.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-410: GENERAL FUND GOVERNING BODY SUMMARY
PURPOSE
The City of Henderson is structured as a Council-Manager form of government pursuant to the North
Carolina General Statutes. The Mayor is elected at-large and eight Council Members are elected to
represent four (4) wards. Each ward has one council member elected by ward vote only and one council
member is elected at-large. Council members and the mayor are elected for four-year staggered terms.
The City Council is the legislative board of City government and sets the policies for the City. It appoints
the City Manager, City Attorney and City Clerk.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Strategic Plan: Council held a Strategic Planning Retreat in February 2022.

•

Infrastructure:
o Continued progress on the Kerr Lake Regional Water Plant upgrade design and future
construction
o CIP projects underway including Water/Sewer Financial Analysis
o 1,680 tons street overlay work completed

•

Major Accomplishments:
o Received a CDBG Grant for Redevelopment
o Received a PARTF Grant for Recreation
o Received a URA Grant for Urban Redevelopment
o As a result of the 2020 Census, redistricting was accomplished for Council Wards
o Downtown pavilion
o Support for the acquisition and development of the NCDOT S-Line and SA-Line
o Named the Operations and Service Center in Honor of Dr. Andrea S. Harris
o Named the Orange Street Parking Lot for Dr. Steven Pearson
o Named Fox Pond Tennis Courts for Ms. Mary Lloyd Barbara Hodges
o Names Fox Pond Shelter #1 for Mr. Eddie Hicks
o Premium Pay increases for employees
o Economic Incentive with MR Williams
o Approved FY Ending June 30, 2021 Audit
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

•
•
•
•
•
•

Continue to increase tax base and stability of existing housing stock
Retaining staffing and competitive salaries
Fire Station #3
IT needs
Redevelopment
KLRW Improvements

10-414: GENERAL FUND CITY ATTORNEY SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide legal counsel to the Mayor, City Council, City Manager and City staff; review
Ordinances and Resolutions as well as contracts and agreements; and to represent the City as
needed.
The City Attorney, D. Rix Edwards, provides part-time services to the City via a contract with
the firm of Stainback, Satterwhite, & Zollicoffer.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• Additional funding for legal counsel outside the realm of the City Attorney’s expertise
• Additional funding for extensive title searches performed by outside contractors.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-420: GENERAL FUND ADMINISTRATIVE SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To guide, lead and direct all City departments in administrative matters related to and necessary
for implementing City Council’s policy.
The Administration Department consists of the City Manager, Assistant to the City Manager,
Executive Assistant to the City Manager, Administrative Support Specialist and Special Projects
Manager. The Special Projects Manager and the Administrative Support Specialist positions
have been frozen and unfunded for years.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
1. Priority 1. Improve the perception of the city.
2. Priority 2. Attract and retain employees with a focus on succession planning.
3. Priority 3. Encourage housing stock that is diverse, safe, and affordable.
4. Priority 4. Promote economic development/develop and revitalize downtown.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•
•
•
•
•

Successfully met all U.S Treasury reporting requirements for receipt of American Rescue
Plan Act (ARPA) funds.
Adopted the West End Urban Redevelopment Plan.
Received a $750,000 Community Development Block Grant (CDBG) from the NC
Department of Commerce to rehabilitate several homes in disrepair as part of the City’s
revitalization efforts.
Began construction of the Williams/Montgomery Street Park utilizing grant funding from the
National Fitness Campaign and the NC Park and Recreation Trust (PARTF) grant program.
Received $3.5 million in federal funding secured by Congressman G.K. Butterfield.
for the Kerr Lake Regional Water System.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

•
•
•
•
•

Addressing the disparities in hiring salaries for various City departments.
Identifying a location and funding source for the construction of a 3rd Fire Station.
Finding additional funds for upcoming capital needs related to building and infrastructure.
Identifying funding sources for the implementation of the City’s redevelopment efforts.
Securing additional funding to fill the shortfall for the Kerr Lake Regional Water System
upgrade and expansion project.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-425: GENERAL FUND HUMAN RESOURCES SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To plan and manage programs related to the recruitment, benefits, classification and
compensation, training, employee safety, and employee and labor relations functions of the City.
Each function should be performed in accordance with the City of Henderson’s policies and
procedures as well as local, state and federal regulations.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Expand the employee wellness program for City employees.
To expand the wellness program so that it will promote healthy lifestyles and
enhance the physical, mental and emotional health of employees.

Goal 2:
Objective 1:

Identify a personnel/payroll system.
To identify a personnel/payroll system that will allow Human Resources staff to
maintain employee statistical data and run reports.

Goal 3:

Expand recruitment efforts to attract and retain qualified applicants for vacant
positions.
To be creative and consider hiring applicants with education and limited
experience or vice versa as trainees until they meet the minimum requirements.

Objective 1:

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

•
•

Effective July 1, 2021, Mark III Employee Benefits began serving as the City of
Henderson’s Benefits Broker. Participated in the entire transition process which included
Request for Proposals (RFPs); coordinated and presented at benefits meetings to
employees and pre-65 retirees; received training on new benefits database and billing
systems; began working with new Medicare Sales Consultant, etc.
A Consultant was hired to provide awareness and training to City employees on
Diversity, Equity and Inclusion (DEI). Collaborated with another Department Head and
worked with the Consultant to schedule meetings and focus groups for employees.
Worked with City Attorney and prepared written documentation and coordinated
telephone hearings for unemployment compensation claims filed by former employees.
For each claim, the adjudicator’s determination was reversed, and claimants were
disqualified for unemployment benefits.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

•

Recruitment: There is difficulty in attracting and retaining employees in some positions
due to salaries that are not competitive to those of other agencies.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-440: GENERAL FUND FINANCE SUMMARY

PURPOSE
To administer the fiscal affairs of the City in compliance with local, state and federal regulations and in
accordance with generally accepted accounting principles of governmental accounting and the rules and
regulations of the North Carolina Local Government Commission. The Finance Department’s
responsibilities include the collection and disbursement of funds, control of expenditures, cash and asset
management, payroll and purchasing. The Department is responsible for insuring that all of the City’s
disbursements are in strict compliance with laws and regulations and the City’s budget ordinance.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Earn the GFOA Certificate of Achievement Award for Excellence in Financial Reporting.
Work closely with auditors to ensure that the FY22 Comprehensive Annual Financial
Report meets or exceeds the requirements for the GFOA award and assist with
preparation of the FY23 budget in compliance with GFOA guidelines.

Goal 2:
Objective 1:

To record and post financial data in a timely and efficient manner.
To utilize and/or expand the comprehensive checklist of critical dates for various items
and/or tasks including revenue receipts (sales tax, utility franchise tax, etc.), debt service
payments, reporting deadlines, etc.

Objective 2:

To continue to review and monitor this checklist on an on-going basis to ensure that
deadlines are met and that financial data is recorded accurately and in a timely manner in
order to provide meaningful and reliable up-to-date financial information to management
and department heads and submit all “outside” reports (Powell Bill, various LGC reports,
NC Dept. of Revenue reports, etc.) by their required due dates.

Goal 3:
Objective 1:

Improve current departmental processes and procedures and cross-train all Finance staff.
Review current processes and procedures for potential improvements. Determine if
distribution of departmental tasks and responsibilities are assigned in the most efficient
manner and re-align/re-assign duties and responsibilities where efficiencies could be
improved.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS

•

Audit: Received a “clean,” unmodified opinion on the FY21 financial audit report. Due to new
GASB 87 rule regarding the required reporting of lease agreement activity in the financial statements,
the City will comply via engagement with Debtbook, Inc. to satisfy this mandate.

•

Automated procedures surrounding electronic procurement and related accounting processes
generated over $63,000 in rebate revenue for the City since its inception. In FY22, we are expecting
to approach $75,000 in accrued revenue. Not only do these innovations build revenue, but
significantly reduce costs and time in processing the City’s expense budgets.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-450: GENERAL FUND INFORMATION SERVICES SUMMARY

PURPOSE
To provide support, assistance, and development to all departments regarding Information
Technology systems.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal:
To update and maintain the City’s Information Technology (IT) infrastructure.
Objective 1: Meet and exceed the needs of the City’s workforce to operate, serve, and protect
the public, in a timely manner.
Objective 2: To keep the City’s critical systems and sensitive information from digital attacks.
Objective 3: Improve upon the existing IT disaster recovery plan.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•
•
•
•
•
•

NCOL has been retained on a two-year contract to provide on-site IT Administration
New firewalls were purchased and installed for all locations to heighten security and
standardize the network.
IT ticketing system
Key fob access network
Migration to NCDIT operating domain
Homeland Security review of the City’s IT network has been scheduled.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
•
•

•

Maintain a hardware replacement rate of 20% to facilitate continuous modernization of the
IT system.
Telephone System Upgrade: Currently, the City uses a telephone system where the hardware
and software are no longer supported by the manufacturer. Replacement parts are being
vended from discarded systems. Our telephone technology, if upgraded, would be a voiceover-internet protocol (VOIP) system.
Continue to improve the City’s website, firewalls, viral threat education, virus, and
ransomware protection.

GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-496: GENERAL FUND DOWNTOWN DEVELOPMENT SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To promote, enhance, facilitate and encourage a vibrant, healthy and historically significant downtown historic
central business district. To ensure the Downtown business district is being managed using the four-point approach
of the Main Street program: organization, promotion, design and economic vitality. To lead the Henderson-Vance
Downtown Development Commission, a volunteer member group organized as a 501c3, in Main Street Community
best practices resulting in community transformation and improvement.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Downtown Henderson is the destination for diverse cultural experiences in Vance County.
Increase job opportunities and growth of tax base through expansion of small businesses that are
complimentary or compatible with existing cultural amenities by 10% over the next three years.

Goal 2:

Create an environment that promotes community involvement downtown to build a progressive
environment supporting business and tourism.
Add three new businesses in the next 12 months and increase participation in community
events/festivals by 5% during the next three years. Increase the walking score by 10%.

Objective 1:

Goal 3:
Objective1:
Objective 2:

Enhance the appearance and attractiveness of the central downtown district through structural
improvements to downtown buildings, beautification projects and litter control.
Promote façade improvement projects to downtown buildings by awarding three new Façade
Grants and one Revolving Loan.
Implement the Downtown Planter Project by installing and maintaining ten new flower planters
and provide upkeep for the new and existing planters and gardens. Install additional benches
throughout downtown. Replace the downtown light pole banners with Progress/Duke Energy
compliant banners. Partner with the Aquest Group on the Downtown Heritage Art Walk Project.
Attractive window art/pictures for vacant downtown buildings. Way-finding signs project.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS

•

•
•
•
•

The Henderson Main Street program earned re-accreditation for its work in 2020-21 and maintained National
Main Street Community and NC Main Street Community status. Downtown Development Director completed
all required training and certifications.
Facilitated seven downtown events including Juneteenth, Trick-or-Treat, Live On Young and the Christmas
parade.
Awarded three Façade Grants; Seven new businesses opened downtown; Net 20 new jobs added downtown.
Completed beautification project at Orange Street parklet and renamed it The Dr. Stephen Pearson Park.
Added four new benches downtown.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

•

•

Façade improvements and elimination of warehousing on Garnett Street: There are numerous buildings in
downtown in need of façade improvements to attract businesses and improve the overall appearance of
downtown Henderson. Promotion of the USDA Revolving Loan Fund.
Funding for beautification projects and downtown banners to enhance the downtown appearance and celebrate
the four season sales and marketing efforts of the downtown merchants.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-498: GENERAL FUND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES
PURPOSE
Planning, Community Development & Zoning, along with Code Compliance operates under the
Development Services Department. Planning, Zoning, and Community Development administers land
use regulations for the City of Henderson. Code Compliance ensures that all neighborhoods and business
areas are safe and decent by enforcing the Minimum Housing, Abandoned, Weeded Lot (Nuisances)
ordinances and other applicable codes. Our overall objective is to promote smart growth, development
and economic opportunity for the City and Extraterritorial Jurisdiction.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:

Create and Identify Community Development projects that will produce an Enforcement
housing plan and potential infill housing development.
Objective 1: Identify neighborhoods where city and county vacant properties exist, evaluate the
surrounding housing stock, determine if dwellings are deteriorated or dilapidated and
create a neighborhood plan that can address needs (including infrastructure and
housing) and potentially locate new housing developments.
Goal 2:
Evaluate city neighborhoods to ensure habitable and safe housing stock.
Objective 1: With strategic planning and creating a housing education program, the Code Compliance
Department will continue to enforce the Minimum Housing Code and Abandoned Structure
Code by evaluation of each dwelling within the neighborhoods and responding to
complaints of substandard dwellings.
Goal 3:
Review and amend Minimum Housing and property maintenance codes.
Objective 1: The Minimum Housing Code must be amended to improve the safety and living
conditions of all residents and reduce the blight within the city’s jurisdiction.
Goal 4:
Assist Redevelopment Commission and the Downtown Development Commission in
redevelopment of the downtown and adjacent areas (West End/ Elmwood and Flint Hill
neighborhoods).
Objective 1: In order to deal with blight and the lack of development, the City and others must officially
define / establish a redevelopment zone and encourage / promote the rebuilding of these
blighted areas.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•
•
•
•
•

North Carolina General Statue 160D Zoning / Land Use amendment complete
Comprehensive Land Use Plan completed June 2022.
Community Development Block Grant awarded $750,000
Ordinance 20-31 Increasing the density in the R-6 and R-8 zoning district. Result Berry Street and
Corbitt Road infill development of 6 homes average selling $250,000. The result of this ordinance
has created infill development at Berry St. and Corbitt Rd 6 residential dwellings.
10 Abandoned Structures Demolished
KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• Funding for Unified Development Ordinance (UDO) and demolition / property abatement.

10-500: GENERAL FUND PUBLIC BUILDINGS & GROUNDS SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide funds on a centralized basis for general building maintenance at City Hall, First
National Bank building and former Municipal Building 110 Young St. and associated insurance
and utility costs for these public buildings. City Hall administration is responsible for
administering this budget.

GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal:
Continue to make improvements to public buildings.
Objective 1: Increase worker productivity and safety through a better working environment.
Objective 2: Continue to improve the look and capability of public buildings and thereby
encourage private redevelopment efforts, including promotion of the downtown
district.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Municipal Building (110 Young St.): provided space to the Vance County Arts Council, and
storage space for the non-profit HUBZone Technology Initiative (HTI).

•

First National Bank building (213 S Garnett St.): Purchase of the S-line by NCDOT (Rail
Division) gives prospect to this building as a commuter station and contributes greatly to
prospective downtown redevelopment.

•

City Hall (134 Rose Ave.): CARES Act renovations were completed which addressed many
previously unfunded building issues, including electric door lock system for employees;
ADA compliance; and enhanced internal security through electronic surveillance and a
strategically placed reception station.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
•
•
•

Additional security cameras are needed for various purposes at City Hall
Security fence needed at City Hall along the north side of the west parking lot.
Maintenance and upkeep: Insufficient funds for interior painting and carpet replacement at
City Hall.
• Backup power should be considered, for at least the IT equipment room at City Hall.

10-510: GENERAL FUND POLICE DEPARTMENT SUMMARY
PURPOSE
The prevention of crime, the protection of life and property, and the general preservation of the public peace.

GOALS AND OBJECTIVES
GOAL 1:
Objective 1:
Objective 2:
Objective 3:
Objective 4:

Reduce the overall violent crime rate for Henderson and continue to reduce the overall Part 1
Crime Volume along the current ten-year trend.
Continue to use the Federal Court System to prosecute violent and habitual felons that affect the
City of Henderson.
Continue working closely with the 11th District Attorney’s Office on prosecution and diversion
strategies.
Continue to engage community groups and organizations in unified programs designed to increase
trust and synergy between our citizens and the Police Department.
Utilize the newly installed NIBIN System to submit shell casings for comparison in an attempt to
connect violent crime cases.

GOAL 2:
Objective 1:
Objective 2:
Objective 3:

To reach and maintain full sworn officer staffing
To hire personnel to fill all existing allocated sworn positions.
Increase in-service training and training in the areas of supervision and command staff.
Innovatively advertise and recruit a diverse workforce that compliments and projects our values.

GOAL 3:
Objective 1:
Objective 2:

Continue to seek resources to address needed CIP purchases
Continue the upgrades of agency technology and improve IT support.
Maintain the current level of operation, efficiency and expenditures of our individually assigned
Police Department vehicle fleet program.
Fulfill the 25% replacement process set for communication equipment and computers.
Place a repeater within the city limits to ensure public safety and continuous radio contact.

Objective 3:
Objective 4:

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•
•
•

Maintained a comparable Part 1 Crime Rate to the prior years.
Obtained close to $500,000 in grant funding to enhance our technological needs as a city and as an agency.
Successfully proposed and attained competitive salary plan to enhance of recruitment and retention of
employees.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• Larger capital items: Includes replacement vehicles and equipment using General Fund monies.
• Maintenance and Capital Improvements: The Police Department is nearly 20 years old and is in need of
upgrades and repairs. This will help prevent larger cost in the future if these issues are addressed now.
• Technology: Law Enforcement has taken huge steps in technology and it is imperative that we keep up with
this trend. The Police Department will need a specialist in IT to be able to maintain equipment and
communicate with various agencies. A more costly portion of this will be annual fees and data storage.
• Recruitment and Retention: We must cultivate a staff of employees that can see obtainable growth in the
profession, education, and finances. We are a leading agency in North Carolina, and we must be competitive in
our compensation. The profession of law enforcement is struggling to recruit qualified employees.

10-530: GENERAL FUND FIRE DEPARTMENT SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To prevent fires and fire hazards throughout the City, suppression and containment of fires, education and
related initiatives in fire prevention, the provision of rescue and emergency medical services, and
interpretation and enforcement of Chapter V of the State Fire Prevention Code.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:

Objective 1:

Goal 2:
Objective 1:
Goal 3:
Objective 1:
Objective 2:
Objective 3:
Goal 4:
Objective 1:

To comply with NFPA 1710 to provide four-minute response times to commercial and
residential areas west of Dabney Dr. and I-85 which fall outside the 1 ½ mile response
area.
Secure funding for design and construction of a third fire station as recommended by the
2019 Fire Study
To hire remaining 6 firefighters to staff four (4) fire fighters per responding apparatus for
fire suppression/prevention activities as recommended by the 2019 Fire Study.
Secure approval and funding for this recommendation.
To replace apparatus in our fleet that are aging, unreliable and showing signs irreparable
wear.
Replace Ladder 1
Replace Engine 3
Replace 1 small vehicle
To continue to improve the retention of qualified and seasoned personnel at all levels.
Employee compensation must be evaluated and adjusted annually to keep pace with our
competitors.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS

• The department reached out to 100,295 participants regarding public fire education. This was
accomplished through home inspections, seminars, social media, and activities in the schools.
(Increase from last year due to more social media posts)
• 100 % of the required fire inspections were completed.
• Our personnel completed 16,070 man-hours of fire/rescue, EMS, and special training. (This was the
highest year in the last 7 years despite restrictions due to Covid 19).
• Completed comprehensive training in ladder company operations using an established third-party
instructor group.
• Worked closely with local public health officials in managing our personnel that had or were exposed
to covid-19 to best preserve adequate staffing. This has been very challenging.
•
•
•
•

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
Funding is needed for design and construction of a third station to comply with the 2019 Fire Study.
Funding is needed to hire the 6 remaining fire fighters as recommended by 2019 Fire Study.
Funding is needed for apparatus and other vehicles that have already been taken out of service and for
those that have reached their lifespan of frontline service.
Funding is needed to continue the reduction of acute and chronic exposures of fire fighters to
carcinogens and other harmful compounds associated with firefighting activities.

10-545: GENERAL FUND PUBLIC WORKS ADMINISTRATION SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide for the overall fiscal and compliance management and support functions for the
Cemetery, Sanitation, Garage, Street, Water Distribution, Sewer Collection and Sewer I&I
Divisions.
This department manages the budget for the Operations and Service Center. Sixty (60%) percent
of the total cost associated with the Operations Center is reimbursed by the Water and Sewer
funds. Additionally, this office (as part of Sanitation Division Management) is charged with
contract compliance responsibilities, and complaint resolution, for any contract or franchise
agreements with private waste haulers or collectors operating within the city, including
commercial/industrial bulk container collection and monitoring customer service.

GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Provide overall management of contracts and other fiscal matters.
Objective 1: Track spending, checking for compliance with contracts and customer service,
and collecting and analyzing data to improve efficiency and track customer
service issues.
Goal 2:
Further development of graphs and other data relative to revenues.
Objective 1: Consistent monitoring of revenues to ensure budget projections are met.
Goal 3:
Improve record keeping.
Objective 1: Continue with training in use of the work order system.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Monitored preventative maintenance measures on equipment.

•

Worked in conjunction with Customer Service in completing review of billing by Waste
Industries resulting in savings and additional revenue for carts not being billed.

•

Continued to utilize more data in tracking expenditures and monitoring contracts for
compliance. As well as tracking expenses within all budgets of the Public Works
Department.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-555: GENERAL FUND GARAGE SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide maintenance and repair services and dispense fuel, oil and other parts and supplies to the
City’s vehicle fleet.
Departmental expenses are “charged back” to the appropriate operations, included (for the most part) in
the individual expenditure accounts of Maintenance and Repair – Equipment (5016-05), Maintenance and
Repair – Auto Repair and Maintenance (5031-05) and Motor Fuels (5031-06). This has the effect of
artificially increasing the size of the General Fund Reserves including the Garage.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Provide quality preventative maintenance to vehicles and equipment in a timely manner.
Documentation of time and materials associated with repairs and maintenance performed
to reduce major repairs and extend the longevity of the equipment.

Goal 2:
Objective 1:

Further development of reporting, record keeping and inventory control.
Consistent monitoring work to ensure accurate monthly and yearly reports.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
• Preventative Maintenance: Continued to improve preventative maintenance on equipment, which

has helped reduce downtime and cost of repairing the equipment.
• Snow Equipment: Very instrumental in keeping our snow removal equipment going during the

January 2022 snow events.
• Leaf Equipment:

Continued maintenance and repair on our leaf machines lead to a safe and
productive leaf season.

• Vehicle Lift: New 20,000-pound capacity vehicle lift installed in the garage will allow work on our

heavier service trucks.
• Fuel Master System: Upgrade to our fuel system will be more reliable and easier to use at the pump

and for administrators.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• Motor Fuels Costs: Unleaded and Diesel fuel costs remain volatile and unpredictable. The City

tracks fuel cost with two different vendors to ensure we receive the best price.
• Aging Fleet: Maintenance and repair costs are increasing as the fleet ages, particularly in the General

Fund.

10-570: GENERAL FUND STREET SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide for the general maintenance of streets, curbs, gutters, sidewalks, storm drains and
ditches throughout the city including snow removal as needed. The City has approximately 80
miles of City-maintained streets and 17 miles of State-maintained/owned roadways.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Provide further extensive training to all Street Division employees.
Objective 1: To ensure that employees perform their jobs in a safe and high-quality manner to
professional standards.
Goal 2:
Identify a comprehensive list of needed storm drain repairs.
Objective 1: Ensure problems are prioritized so that the integrity of the streets is not
compromised and remain safe for travel.
Goal 3:
Identify and prioritize sidewalk repair/replacement projects.
Objective 1: To encourage pedestrian traffic by improving the appearance and safety of
sidewalks.
Goal 4:
Improve appearance of city streets.
Objective 1: To foster a sense of pride in the community as well as encourage citizens to
maintain their homes and surroundings in a well-kept manner.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Sidewalk Repair: The City is continuing to repair and replace broken sidewalk. The street
department is prioritizing and preparing to replace more sidewalks in the coming months.

•

Storm Drainage Improvements: City staff continues to clean and repair storm sewer and
catch basins and has reworked numerous drainage ditches.

•

Asphalt Hot Box: 4-ton capacity hot box will keep asphalt hot with the used of diesel fired
burner. This allows for paving during cold weather and after hours.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

•

Additional Personnel: Would be responsible for maintaining and picking up litter within
street right-of-way’s to improve the appearance of the City. A small amount of part-time
funds are included.

•

Aging Fleet and Equipment: Exisiting fleet is getting older and needs to be replaced. We
are proposing several new vehicles and other equipment for the upcoming year.

FY 22-23 BUDGET

10-580: GENERAL FUND SANITATION SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide solid waste collection and management for approximately 5,400 residential
customers. This includes loose leaf collection (at curbside) during the fall and early winter
months, recycling waste and the bulk item collection program.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:

Improve consistency of fleet by ensuring that each truck follows the same path
every time they run a particular route.
Objective 1: Improved customer service and consistency with pick up times for citizens.
Goal 2:
Improve Communication with citizens relative to sanitation collection procedures.
Objective 1: Educate citizens to help with the efficiency in the collection of yard debris and
recyclables, collection times and to continue to improve the overall appearance of
the City.

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Leaf Collection: Provided loose-leaf collection throughout the city in a timely manner.
Collected 760 tons of leaves from October 15 – January 31.

•

Yard Debris and Recyclables: Collected 713 tons of yard debris, 332 tons of garbage and
94 tons of recyclable material through the first seven months of the year.

•

Reduced Customer Complaints: Improved communication and productivity among
sanitation division personnel. We continued to improve overall yard debris, recycling, and
bulky waste pickup, in turn reducing customer complaints.

•

Audit: Audit of existing charges and services provided by Waste Industries to ensure
revenues and expenditures are in compliance. This is an ongoing process.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
•

Contract Cost Increase: The contract for Waste Industries includes a 3.6% increase based
on the Consumer Price Index (excluding fuel) for sanitation.

•

Aging Fleet and Equipment: Existing sanitation fleet is getting older and needs to be
replaced. We are proposing a single operator leaf truck, garbage and pickup truck for the
upcoming year.

10-620: GENERAL FUND RECREATION SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide a variety of recreational opportunities for all segments of the community in addition to providing
management oversight for the Youth Services/Community/Outreach/Community Service and Restitution Program.
Staff support is also provided to (and policy direction is obtained from) the Recreation and Parks Commission. All
costs are funded by the City of Henderson (55%) and Vance County (45%).
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Continue to develop and implement action plans from the recommendations outlined in the
Comprehensive System-Wide Master Plan approved by City Council in June 2014.
Establish program and facility needs for the future.

Goal 2:
Objective 1:

Expand existing youth athletic programs and implement new youth and adult athletic programs.
Request funding to advertise/operate/reinstate the adult athletic programs that were previously cut
from the budget, and funding to implement new youth athletic programs.

Goal 3:

Increase and expand facilities and parks to include diverse leisure activities and indemnities to
increase participation.
Provide opportunities for citizens to enjoy facilities and parks by providing paddle boats, kayaks,
inclusive playground sets, outside fitness and aquatic equipment.

Objective 1:

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
•

Fox Pond Tennis Courts reconstruction CIP Project was completed in 2021. This project will allow
community and school participants the opportunity to play on a new court for the upcoming years.

•

Recreation and Park Department reclassified (2) Full Times positions; the Recreation and Parks Programs
Facilities Supervisor to the Assistant Director and Park Maintenance Worker to Park Maintenance – Crew
Leader.

•

Trail of Terror was held on Thursday October 21, 2022, at Kings Daughter Park 1(600 West Montgomery
Street) from 6:30pm-9:00pm. The cost was $5.00 per car. There was an increase in participation this year.
There were 293 participants in 79 cars with a revenue of $399. In 2020, there were 166 participants in 40
cars with a revenue of $197.

•

Due to COVID-19 pandemic, we did not have football or basketball during FY 2020-21. We were able to
resume our athletic programs during the spring of 2021. The 2021 East Wake Football league had a total
159 participants, which was an increase of 26 from FY 2019-20. Our 2021-22 youth basketball league had
a total of 221 participants, which was an increase of 33 from FY 2019-20.

•

During the Thursday, January 6, 2022, meeting, the NC Park and Recreation Authority announced a second
round of funding (North Carolina Parks and Recreation Trust Fund Grant). The Henderson-Vance
Recreation & Parks was one of several communities to receive funding which will assist with the formation
of the William & Montgomery Street Park.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• Complying ADA requirements, providing funds to make all facilities, parks, and new projects ADA compliant
i.e., trails, parking lots, bridges, sidewalks, tracks etc.

10-622: GENERAL FUND YOUTH SERVICES SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide the following array of services for referred youth to prevent and reduce juvenile crime and deter
commitments to Youth Development Centers.
•
•
•

Community Service/Restitution provides a means where adjudicated delinquents can demonstrate responsibility
by completing Juvenile Court mandated community service/restitution hours at assigned work sites to
compensate the community and/or victims of their offenses.
Project Youth Outreach promotes interpersonal skills addressing conflict resolution, character education,
informal counseling, bullying, school drop-out prevention, healthy choices and recreational outings for youth
referred to the program.
Vance County Teen Court is a diversion program for first time juvenile offenders who admit responsibility for
committing minor misdemeanor offenses. They now have been allowed through new laws to come through
Teen Court for a second case.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES

Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Goal 2:

Objective 1:

Continue to offer Community Service/Restitution, Project Youth Outreach, Bounce Back and
Teen Court for referred youth and their families in Vance County.
Submit program proposals to the NC Department of Public Safety – Division of
Adult Correction and Juvenile Justice, Vance County and United Way of Vance County to secure
funding to sustain services offered through Youth Services.
Serve Juvenile Court referred (top priority) and youth referred from School Resource Officers for
youth at risk for delinquent and undisciplined behaviors through an array of services under Youth
Services.
Give priority placement for juvenile court referrals through juvenile court counselors and program
staff consensus and admit other at-risk youth through school and law enforcement referrals.

Goal 3:
Objective 1:

Enhance professional and personal development of staff.
Division staff will explore training options to obtain at least 40 hours of training for professional
and personal development. Division staff will also obtain certifications for professional and
personal development.

Goal 4:

Utilizing Eaton Johnson Gymnasium area to provide alternative recreation for at-risk youth and /or
teens.
Division Staff will provide trending outreach activities, programs, and events to deter at-risk
participants criminal activity by enhance knowledge in areas of education, job readiness, selfesteem and self-awareness.

Objective:

FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS
• Vance County Administrative Office provided Youth Services Outreach Unit space at Eaton Johnson
Gymnasium area to offer juvenile and outreach services to their participants.
• Continued funding: Youth Services received funding by NC Department of Public Safety – Division of Adult
Corrections and Juvenile Justice and Vance County for FY 2022-23 to offer Youth Services programs
• Bounce Back, an enhancement of Project Youth Outreach, is a program for middle and high school students on
short-term suspension or credit recovery in the Vance County School System. There were 34 participants in the
program.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES
• State funds controlled by General Assembly and NC General Statue: 100% reimbursable by Vance County via
State allocations and County’s matching funds.

FY 22-23 BUDGET
GOVERNMENTAL FUNDS
10-623: GENERAL FUND AYCOCK AQUATICS CENTER SUMMARY
PURPOSE
To provide a variety of educational, recreational, and athletic programs including indoor swimming,
indoor volleyball, indoor basketball, indoor walking track, multi-purpose rooms and restroom/dressing
room facilities. This recreational facility is a joint City of Henderson-Vance County project that began in
1996 with the construction of the Aycock Multi-field Complex. The County pays 50% of the debt service
and 45% of the operations cost.
GOALS & OBJECTIVES
Goal 1:
Objective 1:

Provide a safe environment for swimmers.
Conduct in-service training for Lifeguards and meet all health department guidelines for
swimming pool operation. Cross train full-time staff and facility supervisors and get all
staff first aid/CPR/AED certified.

Goal 2:

Expand the program base offered and increase participation in existing programs and/or
rentals.
Continue to offer existing popular programs while researching and implementing new
programs based on customer requests, as well as review current programs to make databased decisions as needed and increase program marketing efforts. While enhancing
program and equipment opportunities for participant and/or renters.

Objective 1:

Goal 3:
Objective1:

Increase accessibility in rental space at the Center.
Provide accessible restrooms on the 2nd floor to increase rental opportunities for those
with mobility difficulties.
FY22 ACCOMPLISHMENTS

•
•
•

•

Replacing Dectron Unit for the Aquatic Center, allow the participations to enjoy a clean and safe
environment through general maintenance.
Several new and innovative programs were developed and maintained i.e.. Crafty Kids, Kids Gym,
Aqua Egg Hunt, Pumpkin Splash and Pirate Night and a host of other one day Special Events.
Summer camp was reinstated after the COVID-19 shutdown. Camp was held June 14, 2021 – August
13, 2021, with 42 registered participants. Summer camp attendance averaged 34 campers per week.
Campers enjoyed arts and crafts, field trips, swimming, and outdoor play. Campers also had the
opportunity to receive swim lessons during camp.
Utilizing CARES Act Funding to enhance the facilities going completely touchless with toilets, sinks
and urinals. Also updating the lobby area for a more efficient, safe, secure entrance for customer
service to be provided with access control system.
KEY BUDGET ISSUES

• Adding (1) Full Time Program Specialists to assist with the increasing participation of all programs,

activities and special events.
• Increase staffing to allow for adequate coverage at greenspace facilities and outside rental

opportunities.
• Increase budget to provide more special events to the public.
• Increase pool activity and usage by providing an inflatable water obstacle course.

PURPOSE
This budget is designed to account for the appropriation and transfer of funds directly from the
City to Vance County and the Perry Memorial Library to jointly fund programs that benefit both
the citizens of Vance County and the City of Henderson.

KEY BUDGET ISSUES
•

•

joint operation.

•

•

elections to Vance County.

PURPOSE
The purpose of the Non-Departmental Budget is to provide a system to account for items of a
general nature that apply to all General Fund Departments, but which can not be easily
assignable into specific cost components for particular operations.
The City's retiree insurance is budgeted in Non-Departmental and includes total cost City-wide.
The cost for Enterprise Fund retiree insurance is made by a transfer from those funds to the
General Fund. Pay increases for General Fund employees is budgeted in Non-Departmental.

PURPOSE
This budget is designed to account for the appropriation and transfer of funds directly from the
City to various affliated agencies. Each of these organizations/agencies supplement the services
and programs provided by the City. Their funding meets the “public purpose” definition as
outlined in the North Carolina General Statutes and their continuing eligibilty is periodically
reviewed by the City Attorney and/or an independent Auditor. Most are also supported, to some
extent either by contractual arrangement with the City or through general practice, by Vance
County.
